And it can all happen in one sitting, pretty quickly, without me really planning for it to turn into that.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say out loud, because if you asked me earlier in the day, “Would you eat all of that tonight?” I would say absolutely not. I wouldn’t even think I could. But at night, once it starts, it’s like the amount gets away from me before I’ve fully registered what I’m doing.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo these episodes of losing control over eating happen only at night, or have you noticed similar experiences during other parts of the day as well?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 40 / marketing_manager / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Loss_of_Control\nmanifestation: \"Once I start, especially after a tense night with my husband, it feels like the decision-making part of me goes offline and I keep reaching for more even while I’m thinking, ‘Patricia, stop, this is enough.’\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s mostly at night. I mean, I can overeat at other times in a kind of normal way, like if there’s food around at work or I’m distracted, but the really scary losing-control feeling… that seems tied to nighttime.

Especially after things feel tense with my husband. That’s when it’s like I go from being upset and wound up to suddenly being in the kitchen, and once I start, I don’t feel like I’m making clear choices anymore. It’s not just, “Oh, I want a snack.” It’s more like the part of me that would normally stop and think goes offline.

I’ll literally be thinking, “Patricia, stop. This is enough. You don’t need more,” and at the same time I’m still reaching for something else. That’s the part that feels different from daytime eating. During the day I might make poor choices, but I usually still feel like I’m deciding. At night, in those moments, it feels like I’m not fully in control once it gets going.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.81, F50.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, thank you for coming in. Can you tell me what’s been bringing you here today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess what finally made me make the appointment is that my eating has gotten kind of out of hand at night. After everyone goes to bed, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to have a little bit of leftovers, like just clean up what’s in the fridge, and then somehow it turns into this whole thing. \n\nLike, I’ll start with leftover catered pasta from work, and then suddenly I’ve eaten half the tray. And then I’ll remember there’s dessert from a client dinner, and I’ll have that too, and then I’m opening the pantry and eating whatever snacks are there. It all happens in one sitting, and afterward I’m just sitting there realizing how much I ate. It feels embarrassing even saying it out loud, but that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat a lot after you wake up during the night, like getting up specifically to eat? Or does most of your eating happen in the evening before bed?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate it, honestly. I mean, I don’t usually think of it as waking up in the middle of the night with the purpose of eating, like I’m asleep and then suddenly I’m up because I need food. It’s more that I’m still awake later than I should be, or I’ve gone to bed but I’m kind of restless and end up back downstairs.\n\nMost of the time it starts in the evening, after things get quiet and everyone’s gone to bed. That’s when I feel like I can finally be alone, and then I end up in the kitchen. But I wouldn’t say I’m regularly waking from sleep specifically to eat, if that’s what you mean. It’s more before bed, or around bedtime, and then it just stretches later than I intend.\n\nDoctor: Does this nighttime eating cause you a lot of distress, or does it interfere with your daily life in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does. I mean, I’m still functioning, technically. I get to work, I answer emails, I’m not missing deadlines or anything obvious like that. From the outside I probably look like I’m keeping it together.\n\nBut the mornings after it happens, I feel awful. Not just physically tired, though I do feel drained, but there’s this really heavy shame, like I wake up already disappointed in myself. And then it kind of colors the whole day. I’ll be at work acting normal, sitting in meetings, and part of me is still replaying what I ate the night before and feeling embarrassed.\n\nAnd it’s started making me pull back from things I normally like. Like I’ll plan to go to yoga in the morning, or do some creative time for myself, and then after one of those nights I just cancel it or skip it because I don’t want to face anyone. I don’t want to be seen, even if no one would know. So it’s not like my whole life has fallen apart, but it’s definitely interfering more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself eating a lot at night, do you feel like you can stop whenever you want, or does it feel like you lose control over your eating once it starts?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn’t really feel like I can just stop whenever I want. I mean, I can tell myself that at the beginning — like, “I’m just going to have a little bit and then I’ll go upstairs” — but once it starts, especially if it’s been a tense night with my husband, it’s like the decision-making part of me goes offline.\n\nI’ll actually be thinking, “Patricia, stop, this is enough. You don’t even need any more,” and at the same time I’m still reaching for something else. It’s not like I’m enjoying every bite or making some thoughtful choice. It feels more automatic than that, like I’m watching myself keep going and I can’t quite interrupt it, even though part of me is very aware that I should.\n\nDoctor: When this nighttime eating happens, are you fully awake and aware of what you're doing the whole time?\n\nPatient: I think so? I mean… I’m not sleepwalking, if that’s what you’re asking. I know I’m in the kitchen, I know I’m opening cabinets or the fridge, I know what I’m eating at the time.\n\nBut it’s a little hard to answer because sometimes it feels kind of foggy, not like I’m unconscious, but more like I’m checked out or on autopilot. Like I’m aware enough to know exactly what I’m doing, and I remember it afterward, but in the moment I’m not really pausing and making clear decisions. It’s almost like I’m watching myself do it, which sounds strange, but I don’t mean that I black out or don’t remember.\n\nSo yes, I’m awake and aware, just… not very present, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Has this nighttime eating ever made you consider avoiding social situations or responsibilities during the day out of fear that you might have an episode the next night?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I avoid things because I’m afraid I’ll have an episode later that night, exactly. It’s more like… after it happens, the next morning I feel so drained and ashamed that I don’t want to be around people.\n\nI still get through work. I’m not calling out or missing major responsibilities, and I can put on the professional version of myself enough to answer emails and sit in meetings. But it takes so much more effort than it should, because underneath I’m kind of carrying this embarrassment around all day.\n\nWhere I do notice it more is with the extra things, the things that are supposed to make me feel better. Like I’ll have planned to go to yoga, or I’ll have set aside time to do something creative for myself, and then after one of those nights I just… don’t go. I cancel or I skip it because I don’t want to face anyone, even if no one would know anything happened. It’s like I feel exposed somehow. So it’s not that I’m completely dropping my responsibilities, but I am pulling back from parts of my life.\n\nDoctor: When you're in the kitchen at night eating, do you feel like you're eating rapidly, almost without thinking, like you can't quite control it even though you know you should stop?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. It’s not like sitting down with a plate and having a meal. I’m usually standing at the kitchen island, or sort of leaning against the counter, and it happens fast — embarrassingly fast.\n\nSometimes I’ll be scrolling through work emails on my phone at the same time, like I’m pretending I’m doing something normal or productive, but really I’m just… eating. And I barely taste it. I’ll open something and think, “Okay, just a little,” and then before I’ve really even registered what I’m eating, I look down and the container is empty. Or close to empty. And then there’s this awful moment where I’m like, wait, how did that happen already?\n\nIt feels very automatic. I know I should stop, and I’m aware enough to know I’m eating too quickly, but I’m not really slowing down or savoring it or even making choices in a normal way. It’s more like my hands are moving before my brain catches up.\n\nDoctor: After these nighttime eating episodes, do you ever make yourself sick, use laxatives, exercise excessively, or fast to try to compensate for what you've eaten?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really think so, no. I mean, not in the way you’re describing.\n\nI don’t make myself throw up or anything like that. And I’m not taking laxatives afterward. Exercise-wise, I might have the thought the next day of, “I should do something to undo this,” or I’ll feel like I need to be stricter with myself, but I wouldn’t say I’m exercising excessively. Honestly, sometimes I’m so tired or ashamed afterward that I do less, not more.\n\nWith fasting… I don’t know. I wouldn’t call it a deliberate fast exactly. There are mornings where I feel so disgusted with myself that I don’t want breakfast, or I tell myself I should “reset” and be good that day. But it’s not like a structured plan where I’m not eating for a whole day to compensate. It’s more guilt and trying to feel back in control, if that makes sense.\n\nSo, no, I’m not doing the more extreme things you listed. It’s more that I beat myself up mentally afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel physically hungry when these nighttime eating episodes start, or do you find yourself eating even when you’re not really hungry?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I don’t always pause long enough to check in with myself. It’s not usually like a clear, normal hunger where my stomach is growling and I think, “I need dinner” or something. A lot of the time it feels more like I just find myself in the kitchen and the eating has already started before I’ve really asked myself if I’m hungry.\n\nSometimes I tell myself I’m hungry, or I’ll think, “Well, maybe I didn’t eat enough earlier,” but honestly I don’t know if that’s always true or if I’m just trying to make it make sense afterward. The hunger signal feels kind of… blurred at night. I’m tired, my guard is down, and it’s like the usual decision-making part of my brain isn’t fully online.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: I don’t know that physical hunger is what’s driving it most of the time. I may have some appetite, but it doesn’t feel like simple hunger. It feels more automatic than that.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall specific times when you felt like you couldn’t control yourself once you started eating at night — like you had to keep going even though you didn’t really want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I can think of that very clearly. It usually happens after a really tense evening with my husband — not necessarily some huge dramatic fight, but that tight, awful kind of tension where we’re not really talking normally, or I’m replaying something he said, or I feel criticized or alone. And then once I’m in the kitchen and I start eating, it’s like something switches off.\n\nI know that sounds dramatic, but it really does feel like the decision-making part of me goes offline. I’ll have the thought — very plainly — like, “Patricia, stop. This is enough. You don’t even want this anymore.” And then my hand is still reaching for more. I’ll put something back and then take it out again. Or I’ll tell myself, “Okay, this is the last bite,” and then two minutes later I’m opening something else.\n\nIt’s not that I’m enjoying it by that point. That’s what’s so upsetting. There’s a point where I’m almost watching myself continue, and I don’t want to keep going, but it feels like I have to, or like stopping would make all the feelings rush back in at once. I can hear myself in my head trying to argue for stopping, but it doesn’t seem to matter in the moment.\n\nDoctor: During these nighttime eating episodes, would you say you’re eating a large amount of food in a short period of time — like finishing a whole container or a big portion very quickly, even if you didn’t plan to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the part that scares me the most, because it isn’t just like I have an extra snack or a normal plate of leftovers. I’ll tell myself, very specifically, “I’m just going to have a little bit of the pasta from the fridge,” or “I’ll just take one of the desserts home from the client dinner,” and then somehow it becomes… much more than that.\n\nLike, I came in because there have been nights after everyone else is in bed where I’ll start with leftovers, and then I realize I’ve eaten half a tray of catered pasta — not a small serving, like really a lot of it — plus the dessert that was left from a client dinner, and then I’m still going into the pantry for chips or crackers or whatever snack foods are there. And it can all happen in one sitting, pretty quickly, without me really planning for it to turn into that.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say out loud, because if you asked me earlier in the day, “Would you eat all of that tonight?” I would say absolutely not. I wouldn’t even think I could. But at night, once it starts, it’s like the amount gets away from me before I’ve fully registered what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of losing control over eating happen only at night, or have you noticed similar experiences during other parts of the day as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at night. I mean, I can overeat at other times in a kind of normal way, like if there’s food around at work or I’m distracted, but the really scary losing-control feeling… that seems tied to nighttime.\n\nEspecially after things feel tense with my husband. That’s when it’s like I go from being upset and wound up to suddenly being in the kitchen, and once I start, I don’t feel like I’m making clear choices anymore. It’s not just, “Oh, I want a snack.” It’s more like the part of me that would normally stop and think goes offline.\n\nI’ll literally be thinking, “Patricia, stop. This is enough. You don’t need more,” and at the same time I’m still reaching for something else. That’s the part that feels different from daytime eating. During the day I might make poor choices, but I usually still feel like I’m deciding. At night, in those moments, it feels like I’m not fully in control once it gets going.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

And it can all happen in one sitting, pretty quickly, without me really planning for it to turn into that.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say out loud, because if you asked me earlier in the day, “Would you eat all of that tonight?” I would say absolutely not. I wouldn’t even think I could. But at night, once it starts, it’s like the amount gets away from me before I’ve fully registered what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of losing control over eating happen only at night, or have you noticed similar experiences during other parts of the day as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at night. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I think that does happen. It’s harder for me to separate out, because in my mind the worst episodes are the ones where the amount gets big, but the loss of control can start before it’s “a lot,” if that makes sense.

Like, once I start eating at night — especially after one of those tense evenings with my husband — it can feel out of my hands even if I’m not yet eating some huge amount. I’ll be standing there with something small, or telling myself it’s just a bite or just a little snack, and almost immediately there’s this feeling like the decision-making part of me has just… gone quiet. I’m still aware enough to know I don’t really want to keep going, but I’m not able to stop in the way I normally would.

I’ll literally be thinking, “Patricia, stop, this is enough,” and it’s like that thought is happening in one part of my head while my body is doing something else. I’m still reaching for more, still opening another thing, still going back even after I’ve had that moment of, “Okay, stop now.” So yes, even if the amount weren’t technically enormous, that feeling of not being able to control it is there once it gets started.

It all happens in one sitting, and afterward I’m just sitting there realizing how much I ate. It feels embarrassing even saying it out loud, but that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat a lot after you wake up during the night, like getting up specifically to eat? Or does most of your eating happen in the evening before bed?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate it, honestly. I mean, I don’t usually think of it as waking up in the middle of the night with the purpose of eating, like I’m asleep and then suddenly I’m up because I need food. It’s more that I’m still awake later than I should be, or I’ve gone to bed but I’m kind of restless and end up back downstairs.\n\nMost of the time it starts in the evening, after things get quiet and everyone’s gone to bed. That’s when I feel like I can finally be alone, and then I end up in the kitchen. But I wouldn’t say I’m regularly waking from sleep specifically to eat, if that’s what you mean. It’s more before bed, or around bedtime, and then it just stretches later than I intend.\n\nDoctor: Does this nighttime eating cause you a lot of distress, or does it interfere with your daily life in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does. I mean, I’m still functioning, technically. I get to work, I answer emails, I’m not missing deadlines or anything obvious like that. From the outside I probably look like I’m keeping it together.\n\nBut the mornings after it happens, I feel awful. Not just physically tired, though I do feel drained, but there’s this really heavy shame, like I wake up already disappointed in myself. And then it kind of colors the whole day. I’ll be at work acting normal, sitting in meetings, and part of me is still replaying what I ate the night before and feeling embarrassed.\n\nAnd it’s started making me pull back from things I normally like. Like I’ll plan to go to yoga in the morning, or do some creative time for myself, and then after one of those nights I just cancel it or skip it because I don’t want to face anyone. I don’t want to be seen, even if no one would know. So it’s not like my whole life has fallen apart, but it’s definitely interfering more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself eating a lot at night, do you feel like you can stop whenever you want, or does it feel like you lose control over your eating once it starts?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn’t really feel like I can just stop whenever I want. I mean, I can tell myself that at the beginning — like, “I’m just going to have a little bit and then I’ll go upstairs” — but once it starts, especially if it’s been a tense night with my husband, it’s like the decision-making part of me goes offline.\n\nI’ll actually be thinking, “Patricia, stop, this is enough. You don’t even need any more,” and at the same time I’m still reaching for something else. It’s not like I’m enjoying every bite or making some thoughtful choice. It feels more automatic than that, like I’m watching myself keep going and I can’t quite interrupt it, even though part of me is very aware that I should.\n\nDoctor: When this nighttime eating happens, are you fully awake and aware of what you're doing the whole time?\n\nPatient: I think so? I mean… I’m not sleepwalking, if that’s what you’re asking. I know I’m in the kitchen, I know I’m opening cabinets or the fridge, I know what I’m eating at the time.\n\nBut it’s a little hard to answer because sometimes it feels kind of foggy, not like I’m unconscious, but more like I’m checked out or on autopilot. Like I’m aware enough to know exactly what I’m doing, and I remember it afterward, but in the moment I’m not really pausing and making clear decisions. It’s almost like I’m watching myself do it, which sounds strange, but I don’t mean that I black out or don’t remember.\n\nSo yes, I’m awake and aware, just… not very present, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Has this nighttime eating ever made you consider avoiding social situations or responsibilities during the day out of fear that you might have an episode the next night?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I avoid things because I’m afraid I’ll have an episode later that night, exactly. It’s more like… after it happens, the next morning I feel so drained and ashamed that I don’t want to be around people.\n\nI still get through work. I’m not calling out or missing major responsibilities, and I can put on the professional version of myself enough to answer emails and sit in meetings. But it takes so much more effort than it should, because underneath I’m kind of carrying this embarrassment around all day.\n\nWhere I do notice it more is with the extra things, the things that are supposed to make me feel better. Like I’ll have planned to go to yoga, or I’ll have set aside time to do something creative for myself, and then after one of those nights I just… don’t go. I cancel or I skip it because I don’t want to face anyone, even if no one would know anything happened. It’s like I feel exposed somehow. So it’s not that I’m completely dropping my responsibilities, but I am pulling back from parts of my life.\n\nDoctor: When you're in the kitchen at night eating, do you feel like you're eating rapidly, almost without thinking, like you can't quite control it even though you know you should stop?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. It’s not like sitting down with a plate and having a meal. I’m usually standing at the kitchen island, or sort of leaning against the counter, and it happens fast — embarrassingly fast.\n\nSometimes I’ll be scrolling through work emails on my phone at the same time, like I’m pretending I’m doing something normal or productive, but really I’m just… eating. And I barely taste it. I’ll open something and think, “Okay, just a little,” and then before I’ve really even registered what I’m eating, I look down and the container is empty. Or close to empty. And then there’s this awful moment where I’m like, wait, how did that happen already?\n\nIt feels very automatic. I know I should stop, and I’m aware enough to know I’m eating too quickly, but I’m not really slowing down or savoring it or even making choices in a normal way. It’s more like my hands are moving before my brain catches up.\n\nDoctor: After these nighttime eating episodes, do you ever make yourself sick, use laxatives, exercise excessively, or fast to try to compensate for what you've eaten?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really think so, no. I mean, not in the way you’re describing.\n\nI don’t make myself throw up or anything like that. And I’m not taking laxatives afterward. Exercise-wise, I might have the thought the next day of, “I should do something to undo this,” or I’ll feel like I need to be stricter with myself, but I wouldn’t say I’m exercising excessively. Honestly, sometimes I’m so tired or ashamed afterward that I do less, not more.\n\nWith fasting… I don’t know. I wouldn’t call it a deliberate fast exactly. There are mornings where I feel so disgusted with myself that I don’t want breakfast, or I tell myself I should “reset” and be good that day. But it’s not like a structured plan where I’m not eating for a whole day to compensate. It’s more guilt and trying to feel back in control, if that makes sense.\n\nSo, no, I’m not doing the more extreme things you listed. It’s more that I beat myself up mentally afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel physically hungry when these nighttime eating episodes start, or do you find yourself eating even when you’re not really hungry?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I don’t always pause long enough to check in with myself. It’s not usually like a clear, normal hunger where my stomach is growling and I think, “I need dinner” or something. A lot of the time it feels more like I just find myself in the kitchen and the eating has already started before I’ve really asked myself if I’m hungry.\n\nSometimes I tell myself I’m hungry, or I’ll think, “Well, maybe I didn’t eat enough earlier,” but honestly I don’t know if that’s always true or if I’m just trying to make it make sense afterward. The hunger signal feels kind of… blurred at night. I’m tired, my guard is down, and it’s like the usual decision-making part of my brain isn’t fully online.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: I don’t know that physical hunger is what’s driving it most of the time. I may have some appetite, but it doesn’t feel like simple hunger. It feels more automatic than that.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall specific times when you felt like you couldn’t control yourself once you started eating at night — like you had to keep going even though you didn’t really want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I can think of that very clearly. It usually happens after a really tense evening with my husband — not necessarily some huge dramatic fight, but that tight, awful kind of tension where we’re not really talking normally, or I’m replaying something he said, or I feel criticized or alone. And then once I’m in the kitchen and I start eating, it’s like something switches off.\n\nI know that sounds dramatic, but it really does feel like the decision-making part of me goes offline. I’ll have the thought — very plainly — like, “Patricia, stop. This is enough. You don’t even want this anymore.” And then my hand is still reaching for more. I’ll put something back and then take it out again. Or I’ll tell myself, “Okay, this is the last bite,” and then two minutes later I’m opening something else.\n\nIt’s not that I’m enjoying it by that point. That’s what’s so upsetting. There’s a point where I’m almost watching myself continue, and I don’t want to keep going, but it feels like I have to, or like stopping would make all the feelings rush back in at once. I can hear myself in my head trying to argue for stopping, but it doesn’t seem to matter in the moment.\n\nDoctor: During these nighttime eating episodes, would you say you’re eating a large amount of food in a short period of time — like finishing a whole container or a big portion very quickly, even if you didn’t plan to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the part that scares me the most, because it isn’t just like I have an extra snack or a normal plate of leftovers. I’ll tell myself, very specifically, “I’m just going to have a little bit of the pasta from the fridge,” or “I’ll just take one of the desserts home from the client dinner,” and then somehow it becomes… much more than that.\n\nLike, I came in because there have been nights after everyone else is in bed where I’ll start with leftovers, and then I realize I’ve eaten half a tray of catered pasta — not a small serving, like really a lot of it — plus the dessert that was left from a client dinner, and then I’m still going into the pantry for chips or crackers or whatever snack foods are there. And it can all happen in one sitting, pretty quickly, without me really planning for it to turn into that.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say out loud, because if you asked me earlier in the day, “Would you eat all of that tonight?” I would say absolutely not. I wouldn’t even think I could. But at night, once it starts, it’s like the amount gets away from me before I’ve fully registered what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of losing control over eating happen only at night, or have you noticed similar experiences during other parts of the day as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at night. I mean, I can overeat at other times in a kind of normal way, like if there’s food around at work or I’m distracted, but the really scary losing-control feeling… that seems tied to nighttime.\n\nEspecially after things feel tense with my husband. That’s when it’s like I go from being upset and wound up to suddenly being in the kitchen, and once I start, I don’t feel like I’m making clear choices anymore. It’s not just, “Oh, I want a snack.” It’s more like the part of me that would normally stop and think goes offline.\n\nI’ll literally be thinking, “Patricia, stop. This is enough. You don’t need more,” and at the same time I’m still reaching for something else. That’s the part that feels different from daytime eating. During the day I might make poor choices, but I usually still feel like I’m deciding. At night, in those moments, it feels like I’m not fully in control once it gets going.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself eating even when you weren’t eating a large amount, but still felt like you couldn’t stop or control what you were eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that does happen. It’s harder for me to separate out, because in my mind the worst episodes are the ones where the amount gets big, but the loss of control can start before it’s “a lot,” if that makes sense.\n\nLike, once I start eating at night — especially after one of those tense evenings with my husband — it can feel out of my hands even if I’m not yet eating some huge amount.
